If, on certiorari, the judgment is affirmed, the plaintiff in certiorari shall pay to the defendant all the costs in the supreme or circuit court; if reversed, plaintiff in certiorari shall not be entitled to costs, unless the court so order; if affirmed in part and reversed in part, neither party shall be liable to costs, unless upon express order of the court therefor. 

R.S. 2:33-143. If costs are allowed on certiorari as provided by section 2:33–142 of this title, execution therefor may issue out of the supreme or circuit court. 

R.S. 2:33-144. If a judgment of a small cause court, docketed in a court of common pleas as provided by sections 2:33-101 to 2:33-107 of this title, is reversed upon certiorari and a duly certified transcript of the judgment of the supreme or circuit court is delivered to the clerk of the court of common pleas, such clerk shall file such transcript in his office and enter in the margin of the docket opposite the entry of the judgment the substance, in short form, of the determination on the certiorari.

JUSTICE'S DOCKET 

R.S. 2:33-145. Each justice of the peace, before whom any action is instituted, shall keep a docket, in which he shall enter: a. The names of the plaintiff and defendant;

b. The style and nature of the action, the sum demanded or penalty sued for; c. The time of issuing process, when returnable and the return made thereto by the constable or other officer serving the same; d. The time when a copy of an account or state of demand or set-off was delivered by the parties, or either of them; e. The time of taking the bond or recognizance; f. The time of making or filing any order; g. Adjournment; h. A rule of reference and the report of the referees; i. The jury, when and by whom demanded, and the venire and when issued and how returned; j. The time of trial, the names of the jurors and witnesses, the admission of evidence objected to, the rejection of evidence offered, a description of each paper offered in evidence; k. The verdict and judgment and when given; 

1. The execution or executions, when issued, indorsements thereon and how returned by the constable or other officer; m. The appeal, when and by whom demanded, the date of receiving notice thereof; n. All proceedings had before him touching the action.

No imperfection or irregularity in the docket of a justice shall invalidate any proceedings had before or judgment rendered by the justice, but the same may be corrected at any time to conform to the facts, under rule by the court to certify or otherwise. 

R.S. 2:33–146. Each justice shall, when required, give to either party to a cause a certified copy of the docket entries mentioned in section 2:33-145 of this title, which copies may be used as evidence in any court of this state. 

R.S. 2:33-147. Each justice rendering a judgment

shall make out and enter in his docket a full bill of costs in each cause, specifying each item and the fees therefor, and the amounts paid to him by each party. 

R.S. 2:33-148. A justice, whose term of office has expired or who has resigned, shall, when required so to do, make transcripts from his docket, under his hand and seal, and certify them as "late justice of the peace," and the transcripts shall have the same force and effect and be liable to the same objections as though the justice was still in commission. 

R.S. 2:33–149. Each transcript of a judgment entered in the docket of a small cause court shall bear the impression of the seal of the justice holding the court. 

R.S. 2:33–150. The docket of a justice shall be de posited in the office of the clerk of the county for which he was commissioned, to be there kept as a public record, when: a. He dies, in which case the docket shall be so deposited within one year after his death; or b. He is removed from office by impeachment, or if, either before or after the expiration of his term of office, he is about to remove from the county for which he was commissioned.

R.S. 2:33–151. A justice, whose docket has been deposited as required by section 2:33–150 of this title, or his legal representative, shall, for the purpose of recovering costs which may be due the justice, have free access at all times to the docket so deposited, without payment of any fees to the county clerk therefor. 

R.S. 2:33–152. If a justice, or his executor or administrator, neglects or refuses to deposit his docket as required by section 2:33-150 of this title, he shall forfeit fifty dollars, recoverable in an action at law in a court

of competent jurisdiction by the treasurer of the county, for the use of the county. 

R.S. 2:33-153. A transcript of the record of a cause entered in a docket deposited in the office of a county clerk pursuant to section 2:33-150 of this title, certified to be a true transcript by the clerk, shall be received in evidence in any court of this state with the same effect as if the docket itself were produced. 

ACTIONS ON BILLS OR NOTES R.S. 2:33–154. The holder of a bill of exchange or promissory note may, instead of bringing separate actions thereon in a small cause court against the parties separately liable thereon, include all or any of such parties in one action, and proceed to judgment and execution therein as though all such defendants were joint contractors, subject however to the qualifications and restrictions contained in this article. 

R.S. 2:33–155. The rights and liabilities of the several parties to a bill of exchange or promissory note sued on under authority of section 2:33-154 of this title shall, as between each other, remain as heretofore, saving only the rights of the plaintiff in so far as they may be determined by the judgment in the action. 

R.S. 2:33-156. Plaintiff, in an action authorized by section 2:33-154 of this title, shall annex to his state of demand a notice containing a copy of the bill or note sued on, with its indorsements, and stating that the action is brought to recover the amount due thereon. 

R.S. 2:33–157. A joint drawer, maker, indorser, or acceptor of a bill or note sued on as authorized by section 

2:33-154 of this title may prove in abatement of the action the nonjoinder of any other joint drawer, maker, indorser or acceptor.

R.S. 2:33-158. A defendant to an action commenced under authority of section 2:33–154 of this title may set off his demands against the plaintiff as though he had been sued separately. 

R.S. 2:33–159. If, in an action authorized by section 

2:33-154 of this title, the whole amount of a set-off allowed to a defendant equals the amount allowed to plaintiff, the verdict shall be for such defendant generally; if in excess of the amount allowed to plaintiff, for such defendant for the excess. The verdict shall in all cases certify the amount allowed to each defendant as a set-off. 

R.S. 2:33–160. Judgment in an action authorized by section 2:33-154 of this title may be rendered: a. Upon confession, default or trial, for plaintiff against one or more of the defendants, or for one or more of the defendants against plaintiff, as the rights and liabilities of the respective parties shall appear; b. Against joint contractors, some only of whom have been served with process, which judgment shall have the same effect against the joint contractors as heretofore. 

Plaintiff shall not have judgment against a several drawer, maker, indorser or acceptor not served with process. 

R.S. 2:33–161. Payment of a judgment in an action authorized by section 2:33-154 of this title by a defendant or defendants secondarily liable as between themselves shall not operate as a satisfaction thereof as against a defendant or defendants liable over on the bill or note to the defendant or defendants making such payment. The defendant or defendants making payment shall, upon application to the justice and two days' notice given to the other parties to the judgment, have the full benefit and control of the judgment, subject to such regulations as may be imposed, for the purpose of compelling

repayment from any defendant liable for such repayment. 

Upon such an application, the justice may order an issue to try the question in controversy. 

R.S. 2:33–162. When an execution against goods and chattels issues in an action authorized by section 2:33154 of this title, the constable to whom the execution is directed shall, after levying upon the property liable to execution, and before proceeding to sell the property of the person or persons secondarily liable on the bill or note sued on, make, if possible, the amount of the execution out of the property of the defendant or defendants primarily liable as between themselves for the payment of the bill or note. 

R.S. 2:33-163. For the purpose of enabling the levying officer to proceed as provided by section 2:33–162 of this title, the justice shall indorse on the execution the order in which the defendants are liable as between themselves for the payment of the bill or note sued thereon, according to the terms thereof. 

R.S. 2:33-164. If a judgment in an action authorized by section 2:33–154 of this title is rendered in favor of any defendant, he shall recover his costs against plaintiff as though he had been sued alone. 

ATTACHMENT 

R.S. 2:33-165. Any justice of the peace within this state, on application made and affidavit filed for the purposes stated in article 1 of chapter 42 of this title (R.S. 

2:42-1 et seq.), shall, and is hereby required to issue out of the small cause court, when such court has jurisdiction, an attachment, under his hand and seal, for any sum not exceeding two hundred dollars, directed to a constable, who shall execute the same in the manner pro- 

[Honeyman's Justice Supp.]-4

vided by said article 1 of chapter 42 on the effects, rights and credits of the defendant.

R.S. 2:33–166. After an attachment issues as provided by this article, plaintiff shall forthwith advertise, in three of the most public places in the county, that the attachment has been issued by the justice against the absconding or absent debtor, so that any person having a greater demand against such defendant than is cognizable before a justice of the peace may take out an attachment for the recovery of the same. 

R.S. 2:33-167. Upon the return of the attachment mentioned in section 2:33-165 of this title, the justice shall set a day for a hearing of the cause, not less than twenty days from the issuing of the attachment. 

R.S. 2:33–168. On or before the day set for the hearing of the cause as provided in section 2:33–167 of this title, the plaintiff shall file a copy of his account or state of demand. 

R.S. 2:33-169. On or before the day appointed for the hearing of the cause, defendant may file with the justice a bond to plaintiff, with sufficient surety, in double the amount of plaintiff's claim as stated in the affidavit filed pursuant to section 2:33–165 of this title, and conditioned for the due and safe return of the property seized and taken under the attachment if judgment be rendered for plaintiff, and thereupon the property attached shall be released from the lien of the attachment and restored to defendant.

The bond shall be approved by the justice and filed by him for the benefit of plaintiff. If individual and not corporate surety is given, the surety shall be one or more freeholders and residents in the county in which the attachment issues. 

[Honeyman's Justice Supp.]

R.S. 2:33-170. Defendant, after filing the bond as provided by section 2:33-169 of this title, shall file his answer, copy of account or set-off, if any he have. 

R.S. 2:33-171. A cause commenced by attachment may, when the bond required by section 2:33-169 has been filed, be adjourned, conducted and tried as if it had been commenced by summons as provided by this chapter. 

R.S. 2:33-172. If the defendant files an affidavit setting forth facts which would render the attachment illegal or void, the justice shall, on motion to quash the writ, try the facts alleged, without requiring defendant to file the bond required by section 2:33-169 of this title and give judgment on the motion. 

R.S. 2:33-173. The justice shall, if the plaintiff in the attachment make sufficient proof of the debt due him, give judgment for plaintiff, and issue an execution to the constable against the effects of defendant, as in other causes cognizable before a justice. 

R.S. 2:33-174. The effects of defendant taken shall not, unless perishable, be sold until after the lapse of three months, to enable defendant or some one for him to redeem them. In the meantime the same shall be inventoried and kept in such manner as the justice shall direct. 

R.S. 2:33-175. Either party may appeal from the judgment, in like manner, in every respect, as if the action had been commenced by summons. In case the defendant shall fail to file his bond on or before the day appointed for the hearing of the cause he nevertheless may appeal from such judgment upon giving bond as aforesaid, and thereupon the property attached shall be restored to the defendant and released from the lien of the attachment.

R.S. 2:33–176. When a written claim to goods and chattels taken under a writ of attachment issued pursuant to the provisions of this article is made to the constable in possession thereof by a person other than defendant, the procedure to try and determine such a claim shall, in all respects, be the same as provided by sections 2:33-95 to 2:33-98 of this title for the trial and determination of claims to goods and chattels levied in and taken under writs of execution. 

R.S. 2:33-177. If the effects of defendant taken under attachment are in the possession of a third person, such third person may be proceeded against as garnishee as provided by article 1 of chapter 42 of this title (R.S. 2:42-1 et seq.) of this title. If plaintiff makes sufficient proof of the debt due to him and of the effects, rights or credits in the hands of the garnishee, the justice shall give judgment therein for plaintiff, and award and issue an execution thereon to the constable against the garnishee as in other cases cognizable before a justice. If plaintiff does not make sufficient proof of the property in the hands of the garnishee, he shall pay the garnishee his costs, and the justice shall issue execution therefor. 

R.S. 2:33-178. If a garnishee in attachment proceedings before a justice neglects or refuses to deliver up any money, goods, chattels or effects of defendant in his possession or to pay over to the justice any debt due from him to defendant, plaintiff shall, within sixty days from the day of garnishment, proceed, against the garnishee as herein provided. In default thereof the garnishee shall be discharged from all liability under the garnishment, which shall be void and of no effect. 

R.S. 2:33-179. Upon the expiration of the term of office of a justice before whom a judgment has been ob-

tained against a defendant in attachment, all the proceedings against the garnishee may be had before the other justice. 

R.S. 2:33-180. From a judgment rendered by a justice for or against a garnishee, either party may appeal as provided for appeals in this chapter from judgments of justices in other cases.

II. JUSTICES OF THE PEACE Section 1. Qualifications and Bond. 

2. Authority. 

3. Vacancies. 

§ 1. Qualifications and Bond. "It is of great public importance that only men of character hold the office of  It is to safeguard the public that they are required to give bond. In order to further serve the public interest, regulation is made by statute that previous conviction of crime or misdemeanor will act as a disqualification. Such regulations are in accord with the Constitution of this State."1 

§ 2. Authority. "The authority of a justice of the peace is limited. Historically, his authority, by virtue of his office, was wholly ministerial to prevent breaches of the peace and bring criminals to justice." "And so it remains except as amplified by statute." 19 2 § 3. Vacancies. Where, in a ward in any municipality of this state, an election has heretofore been held to fill a vacancy in the office of justice of the peace and at the election a majority of the votes cast were in favor of having elected a justice of the peace, the provisions of such election are deemed to apply to, and be in operation and force in, such municipality. All such elections have been validated and confirmed, notwithstanding any defect in the notice of the election or in the calling of the election or in the method or manner of the submission or certification thereof.*

§ 4. Appointment. 

A municipality being operated under the commission form of government, the recorder should be appointed for a term of four years, despite the provisions of another statute to the contrary."

An acting recorder, appointed by the resolution of a township governing body, is not disqualified from acting as such merely because he is not an attorney at law. 

He is a de-facto officer, and his acts are valid as to the public and as to third person, though the governing body may have possessed no power to appoint him." 

§ 5. Acting Mayor. An acting mayor, the president of the borough council, may, on the mayor's request and during that officer's absence from the municipality for a period of less than three days, hold the recorder's court." § 6. Term. A recorder's term of office under the 

Towns Act of 1895 is two years, unless the authorities

A recorder in any municipality who has been in a public office or position in the municipality for the period of twenty-five years and has reached the age of sixty years may, on his own request, be retired from service by the body, board, or officer possessing the power to appoint his successor in the event of a vacancy in the office. 8a The retired official is to receive, during his natural life, a pension consisting of such sum as the body or board may determine by resolution, and this pension is to be paid in twelve equal monthly installments.8a § 7. Jurisdiction. A recorder has been said to be a judicial officer vested with the powers conferred by law in criminal matters on a justice of the peace or a policecourt judge. He is a public officer and not a servant or agent of the municipality for whose wrongful act liability may arise under the doctrine of respondeat superior." 

A township recorder lacks the jurisdiction to try an alleged violation constituting a misdemeanor under the autobus legislation.10 In a case where a police recorder gave a person a sentence of six months for attempted suicide, the court held the sentence to be void, because the court lacked jurisdiction: " attempted suicide is a misdemeanor and is punishable by imprisonment for a term of not more than three years and/or a fine, and a recorder possesses the jurisdiction to sentence defendants for crimes only in an instance where the penalty provided for the offense does not exceed six months.11

57 § 8. Record. A conviction by a magistrate in summary proceedings on a penal statute cannot be supported, unless a proper record has been made thereof, disclosing all the prerequisites of a legal trial and conviction.12 

Either there must be proper proof or a plea of guilty must be entered.12 § 9. Bail Bonds. It has been ruled that, in the absence of express statutory authority, a magistrate possesses no power to accept money in lieu of a bail bond.18 

A magistrate who holds money deposited in lieu of bail holds it in trust to return it to the defendant regardless of who has deposited such money, if, by reason of the performance in accord with the terms of the deposit, the defendant becomes entitled thereto.¹ When a town recorder possesses no power to exact bail, there can be no recovery against the town for such officer's conversion of money deposited in lieu of bail;14 if the exaction has been legal, the act is the act of a judicial officer, and no liability can attach to the municipality therefor.14 15 

§ 10. Abolition of Office. The office of borough record er, whether such office be created by statute or by the borough council, may be abolished for purposes of economy. 16 "It is necessary that the duties falling upon a recorder be performed, but it is not necessary that there should be a recorder. The mayor has statutory authority for the performance of all such duties." 16

When the record shows merely that the recorder has been appointed by means of a resolution, his office may be abolished by a resolution.16

§ 11. Ineligibility to Hold Other Offices. Since a re corder is a judicial officer of the municipality, he is not eligible to be a member of the General Assembly of this State.18 

§ 12. Clerk of Recorder in Second-Class Cities. When any person holding the position or office of clerk to the recorder in any second-class city having a population not exceeding one hundred thousand inhabitants, as ascertained by the preceding federal census, has served therein continuously for a period of ten years from the date of his original appointment, he is to continue to hold such position or office during his good behavior.¹9 

Such clerk is not to be discharged, dismissed, or suspended from office or reduced in pay except for inefficiency, incapacity, or other just cause and until he has been furnished with written charges stating the reason for the discharge, dismissal, suspension, or reduction and has been given a reasonable time to make written answer thereto.19 The discharge, dismissal, suspension, or reduction may not be made until the charge or charges have been examined and found true in fact by the municipal governing body, after a hearing on reasonable notice to the person charged at which time he may be represented by counsel and may offer testimony or other evidence in his own behalf.19

8.14. Right to Sue. The injured person is the only one who possesses the right to bring an action against the wrongdoer, unless the injured party assigns his right to another, which act he cannot do prior to the taking of a judgment in a tort action." "A married woman may maintain an action against a third person to recover damages for personal injuries despite the fact that the jury may decide, that her husband with whom she was riding at the time, was guilty of contributory negligence. In the enforcement of her rights against a third person, a married woman stands in the same relation to her husband as a stranger."la 

Uncertainty as to the amount of damages sustained will not preclude a recovery.21b Such is also the rule where the damages are not susceptible of accurate proof or cannot be calculated with exactness or precision, providing that the evidence affords a reasonable basis for the calculation thereof.21b 

§ 15. Actions Based on Statutes. It is a fundamental rule that a right of action can be created by statute.**

§ 17. False Imprisonment. False imprisonment has been defined as the constraint of a person without legal justification.29 Such constraint is actionable. The justification is to be determined by the circumstances existing at the time of the arrest or detention.*9 

§ 18. Deficiency Action. When the proceeds of a re sale under the Uniform Conditional Sales Act do not suffice to defray the expenses thereof, the expenses of retaking, keeping, and storing the goods and chattels, as well as the balance due on the purchase price, the seller may recover the deficiency from the buyer in an action at law.30 In this deficiency suit, however, the buyer is entitled to a credit on the amount found to be due in an amount equal at least to the fair market value of the goods and chattels so sold at the time of the sale thereof.30 

The amount received at any sale authorized by the Act is evidential of such fair market value but is not conclusive proof on such matter. 30 

§ 19. Divisibility of Actions. One cannot divide a sin gle cause of action into several claims and institute separate causes of action on each.31 Separate actions may be maintained only in a case where there exist separate and distinct causes of action.31

As These rules apply to actions predicated on torts. a general rule, a single wrongful act with respect to one individual is the basis for only one cause of action, and only one cause of action may be maintained therefor.39 

A tort may be the basis for separate and distinct causes only in an instance where injury has resulted to both person and property; 32 thus, the court has ruled that a

63 cause of action for personal injuries and for medical expenses and loss of earnings is a single cause of action and is, therefore, not divisible:32 the expenditures for the doctor's services cannot be considered as property damages as distinguished from the injury to the person. 82 Separate actions cannot be maintained for each of several past-due monthly installments of rent. The whole amount due must be recovered in one action.38 "The action for rent is for a breach of contract. The damages to be recovered are the rents due at the time the action is brought. That the obligation, if any, in this case was predicated upon the fact that the tenant had held over beyond the end of the term in no sense altered the legal situation. The contract to pay rent was implied and the damages would be the same as though it were express. 

Those damages, accrued up to the beginning of the suit, constituted one cause of action." 88

VI. ANIMALS Section 20. Injuries Caused by Animals. 

21. Exhibitions. 

22. Food for Live Stock. 

23. Sale of Chicks. 99 84 

§ 20. Injuries Caused by Animals. "The common law requires every person to keep his cattle on his own land and not to trespass on the lands of the other person.' 

For such trespasses compensatory damages may be recovered.84 Where the matter is governed by statute, the plaintiff must bring his case clearly within the statute."4

In a case which concerned the killing of a child by a team of horses, it was held that the owner of an animal, possessing no natural propensity to be vicious, is not responsible for the injuries resulting to another through the animal's vicious acts, unless it be proved that the owner has had knowledge, either actual or constructive, of the vicious disposition of the animal.⁹5 

Should a dog bite a person who is on or in a public place or lawfully on or in a private place, including the property of the owner of the dog, the owner of the dog is liable for the damages suffered, regardless of the former viciousness of such dog or the owner's knowledge of such viciousness. A person may be said to be lawfully on the property of the owner, when he is on such property in the performance of a duty imposed on him by the laws of this State or by the laws or postal regulations of the United States or when he is on such property as a result of the owner's express or implied invitation.86 

When a case of rabies exists among dogs or other do-

§ 21. Exhibitions. No person may keep or exhibit any wild animals, other than birds or fowls, at any road stand, gasoline station, or market located on any of the public streets or highways of this State. The penalty for such an offense is a fine of not more than one hundred dollars to be sued for and recovered with costs in an action at law by the New Jersey Society for the Prevention of Cruelty to Animals.37 § 22. Food for Live Stock. In 1938 an Act was passed with respect to feeding stuffs for live stock and poultry. 

This Act provides, inter alia, that any manufacturer, jobber, importer, or other person is guilty of a violation of the Act and, on conviction thereof, is to pay a penalty of not less than twenty-five dollars and not more than one hundred dollars for the first offense and not less than one hundred dollars and not more than two hundred dollars for each subsequent offense, if he has been guilty of any of a series of acts set forth in the statute.³ 38 § 23. Sale of Chicks. "baby chick" is any domestic fowl under the age of six weeks.40

VII. ANIMALS, CRUELTY TO Section 24. Horses. 

25. Mutilation of Birds or Animals. 

25a. Injuries to Animals Caused by Use of Motor Vehicles. 

§ 24. Horses. A fine of not more than one hundred dollars for each offense is incurred by a person who cuts the tissue, tissues, muscle, or muscles of the tail of a horse or otherwise operates on it for the purpose or with the effect of altering the natural carriage of the tail, excepting in a case where the cutting or operation is necessary for the health or life of the horse, as certified in writing by a duly licensed veterinarian, or who causes, procures, or knowingly permits such cutting or operation to be done, or who assists in, or is voluntarily present at, such cutting or operation. The word "horse" includes a mare or gelding. That one owns or possesses, or shows or exhibits at any horse show or exhibition in this State, a horse the tail of which had been so cut or operated on constitutes a violation of this section, whether the cutting or operation is performed within or without this State.* This section does not apply in an instance where the cutting or operation was performed prior to January 1st, 1936, or where, as evidenced by the affidavit of a veterinarian in conformity with this Act, the cutting or operation has been necessary for the health or life of the horse.46 46 

§ 25. Mutilation of Birds or Animals. The mutilation of the bodies of wild birds or animals results in a penalty

§ 25a. Injuries to Animals Caused by Use of Motor 

Vehicles. A person who is operating a motor vehicle and who knowingly hits, runs over, or causes injury to a dog, horse, or cattle must stop at once and ascertain the extent of the injury.47 Thereupon he must report the matter to the nearest police station or police officer or notify the nearest Society for the Prevention of Cruelty to Animals and give his name, address, operator's license, and registration number as well as the location of the injured animal. To violate any of these provisions results in a fine of not less than five dollars and not more than twenty-five dollars for the first offense and not less than twenty-five dollars and not more than fifty dollars and ten days in jail for the second offense.b

VIII. APPEAL Section 26. Method of Review. 

27. Basis. 

28. Time. 

29. Bond. 

§ 26. Method of Review. When the court has pos sessed jurisdiction, the trial can be reviewed only by means of an appeal.48 In a case where the court has had no jurisdiction, the review may be by appeal or by certiorari.48 § 27. Basis. An appeal may be taken in an instance where the judgment of the lower court is final and does not concern a matter resting in the court's discretion.49 § 28. Time. If an appeal is not taken within the time required by the statute, the proceeding will be dismissed, unless the power to extend the time for the appeal is conferred by the statute.50 § 29. Bond. An appeal from the small cause court will be set aside in a case where the appellant has deposited with the justice of the peace money in double the amount of the judgment and costs instead of a bond for such amount in accord with the statute.51 Money in the hands of the justice might not be available after the rendition of the judgment on the appeal."

IX. APPRENTICES Section 30. Agreement of Apprenticeship. 

§ 30. Agreement of Apprenticeship. The managers, trustees, or governing body of a children's home, to which a child has been indentured in accord with the law, may at any time, with the child's free will and accord, apprentice such child to serve as a clerk, apprentice, or servant in a profession, trade, business, or manual occupation, until the child, if a male, has attained the age of twentyone years or, if a female, has attained the age of eighteen years or for a shorter period of time.5% Such apprenticing is to be effected in the manner provided in the Act in the case of apprenticing by a child's parents.5*

X. ARBITRATION Section 31. Effect of Submission to Arbitration. 

32. Notice of Proceedings. 

33. Termination of Authority. 

§ 31. Effect of Submission to Arbitration. The sub mission of a matter to arbitration constitutes a contract, and the arbitrators' authority is derived from the assent of the parties to the terms of the submission.53 Such parties are bound only to the extent, in the manner, and under the circumstances stipulated, supplemented by the pertinent provisions of the Arbitration Act.58 To submit a litigated question to arbitration effects a discontinuance of the suit in the absence of a stipulation to the contrary.54 

§ 32. Notice of Proceedings. Those who are parties. 

to an arbitration proceeding are entitled to receive notice of the time and place of the hearing to be held by the arbitrators.55 In an instance where an award under an arbitration agreement was made against two parties and where one of the parties received no notice of the hearing and there was no evidence to indicate that notice to the second party, if given, would constitute notice to the first, the confirmation of the award was set aside." 55 

§ 33. Termination of Authority. The expiration of the time prescribed by the parties for the making of the award terminates the authority of the court to direct

55. Kopp v. Grobart, 12 N. J. Misc. 488, 172 Atl. Rep. 733; aff. 114

XI. ASSIGNMENTS FOR BENEFIT OF CREDITORS Section 34. Effect on Rights of Others. 

§ 34. Effect on Rights of Others. Under an assign ment for the benefit of creditors, the assignee's duties are merely directory and are not conditions precedent to the passing of title;57 hence, when a judgment creditor assigns his property prior to the execution of the judgment, the execution is ineffective, even though the assignee has not complied with all the statutory requirements at the time of the execution.57 

57. Thomas Wood & Sons v. Memelaar, 11 N. J. Misc. 823, 168 Atl. 

Rep. 606. 73

XII. ATTACHMENT Section 35. General Rules. 

36. Property Subject to Attachment. 

37. Affidavit. 

38. Levy. 

39. Claim of Property. 

40. Corporations. 

§ 35. General Rules. A 1939 statute provides that an attachment may issue against females whether they be married or sole; corporations not created by the laws of this State and not authorized to do business here; jointstock associations, and corporations not created by the laws of this State but authorized to do business here unless the laws of the state where such a foreign corporation has been created do not permit attachments against corporations created by the laws of this State and authorized to do business in that state." 57a 

Attachment statutes are to be construed liberally in favor of creditors.58

It has recently been held that, where the damages are unliquidated, a writ of attachment cannot be issued in this State.59

A judgment in attachment proceedings in Pennsylvania has no binding effect on the defendant in this State.60 

§ 36. Property Subject to Attachment. To validate an attachment on shares of stock, the statutory method must be followed:61 there must be an actual seizure of the

"For example, money raised on an execution and held by the sheriff cannot, by virtue of an attachment, be taken out of the sheriff's hands; but the sheriff must retain it despite the attachment and pay it into court in the cause in which the execution issued."68 § 37. Affidavit. An attachment being sought on the ground that the defendant is removing his property from the jurisdiction, the affidavit used in the proceedings is insufficient if, entitled in the cause, it is impossible to separate the heading thereof from the body. The affidavit must be such as would be sufficient to justify an order for bail.64 § 38. Levy. A levy under a writ of attachment on an alleged right or credit is not valid, unless the right or credit has actually been in existence at the time of the attempted levy; thus, a levy on a debt which is alleged to be due to the defendant from a third party is invalid, unless the debt has existed at the time of the execution, whether it be immediately demandable or demandable in the future.65

Section 41. Regulations. 

XIII. AVIATION 

42. Liens on Aircrafts. § 41. Regulations. The Legislature passed a statute in 

1938 to regulate aeronautics over and within this State.68 

A wilful violation of any of the provisions of the Act or of any of the rules, regulations, or orders issued pursuant to the Act, other than the provisions, rules, regulations, or orders pertaining to the commission provided for by the Act, is a misdemeanor, punishable by a fine of not more than five hundred dollars or imprisonment for not more than ninety days or both.69 

A person may not fly an aircraft in this State, when he is using, or is under the influence of, intoxicating liquors, cocaine, or other habit-forming drugs;" further, one may not carry passengers who are obviously under the influence of such liquors or drugs.70 To violate this Act is to commit a misdemeanor.71 § 42. Liens on Aircrafts. The law now provides for lines on aircrafts in favor of those who do work thereon or furnish materials in connection therewith.78 This lien is superior to all other liens except those for taxes, the operator of the aircraft being deemed by law to be the agent of any owner, mortgagee, conditional vendor, or other lienor for the purpose of creating such superior lien.72

The procedure to be taken in realizing on the lien is outlined in the statute.73

XIV. BARBERS Section 43. Licenses and Regulations. 

§ 43. Licenses and Regulations. Any person or corpo ration or their agents violating any of the provisions of the Act with respect to the licensing and regulating of barbers is liable to a penalty of not less than five dollars and not more than one hundred dollars and, for the second and each subsequent offense, to a penalty of not less than fifty dollars, to be recovered by, and in the name of, the New Jersey State Board of Barber Examiners." The same penalties apply to a violation of section 26 of the Act, which concerns sanitary regulations.74 

Every district court, justice of the peace, or police magistrate may, on the filing of a duly verified and written complaint (which verification, when made by any member or members or their agents or by a duly authorized employee of the aforesaid Board or, in the case of a violation of any provision of section 26, by any member or duly authorized agent or employee of the Board, may be made on information and belief) to the effect that a person has violated any provision of this Act, issue process at the suit of the Board in the nature of a summons or warrant. Such warrant may issue against the accused without first obtaining an order from the court or judge.74 74 The following form may be used for the complaint in the prosecution of barbers under this Act:

manner, without a jury, to hear the testimony and to determine and give judgment in the matter, without the filing of any pleadings, for the plaintiff for the recovery of the judgment with costs or for the defendant." 

Should the judgment be rendered for the plaintiff, the court is to cause a defendant who neglects or refuses to pay the judgment and the costs and charges incident thereto to be committed to the county jail for a period not exceeding one hundred days.74

All process under this Act is to be served and executed by the officers authorized to do so in such court." 74 The judicial officer possesses the power to adjourn the hearing or trial from time to time, but, excepting in a case where the first process has been a summons, it is the judicial officer's duty to detain the defendant in safe custody, unless he enters into a bond to the plaintiff, with at least one sufficient surety, in double the amount of the penalty claimed, conditioned for his appearance on the day to which the hearing is adjourned and then from day to day until the disposition of the cause and to abide by the judgment of the court. XV. BASTARDY Section 44. Nature of Proceedings. 

45. Jurisdiction. 

46. Parties. 

47. Bond Taken in Proceedings. 

48. Appeal. 

§ 44. Nature of Proceedings. Bastardy proceedings are civil in nature," notwithstanding the fact that under the statute the appeal in such proceedings is to be made to Quarter Sessions, a court of criminal jurisdiction.76 

§ 45. Jurisdiction. The juvenile and domestic court has been held to be without jurisdiction in a bastardy proceeding prosecuted in the name of the child's mother and not in the name of the overseer of the poor." 77 § 46. Parties. The municipality and the putative father are the only proper parties to bastardy proceedings.76 

§ 47. Bond Taken in Proceedings. The magistrate is suing the warrant for the apprehension of the putative father for the purpose of making the statutory adjudication with respect to filiation possesses no authority to accept money in lieu of the appearance bond prescribed by the statute.78 The section of the Criminal Procedure 

Act with regard to accepting money in lieu of a bail bond applies only to criminal proceedings;78 hence, bastardy proceedings being civil in nature, money cannot be taken.78 Where a town recorder took money in lieu of the 

75. Thatcher v. Hackett, 16 N. J. Misc. 459, 1 Atl. Rep. (2d) 438; 

Callaway v. Belleville, 116 N. J. L. 377, 184 Atl. Rep. 819. 

76. Thatcher v. Hackett, supra. 

77. Gallagher v. Smathers, 12 N. J. Misc. 157, 170 Atl. Rep. 224. 

78. Callaway v. Belleville, supra. 83

appearance bond, the town was held not to be liable for the money, the record not having disclosed that the town received the money:78 the recorder having possessed no authority to accept the money, he did not act by virtue of his office in having done so.78

The bond in filiation proceedings, when recorded in the county clerk's office, has the effect of a civil recognizance and becomes a lien on the obligor's land.79 If the bond be not cancelled of record, the instrument remains such a lien during the succeeding twenty years." The judgment based on the bond continues the lien thereof.79 

§ 48. Appeal. The appeal in these proceedings is to be taken, not by the mother, but by the director of welfare of the municipality.80 

78. Callaway v. Belleville, supra. 

79. Price v. State, 121 N. J. E. 436, 190 Atl. Rep. 846. 

80. Thatcher v. Hatchett, 1 Atl. Rep. (2d) 438.

XVI. BEAUTY CULTURE Section 49. Regulation of Hours. 

§ 49. Regulation of Hours. The governing body of any municipality may pass, enforce, alter, or repeal ordinances to regulate the opening and closing of beauty parlors on Sundays and holidays; to regulate the hours of opening and closing on weekdays, and to fix and enforce penalties for the violation of such ordinances.81 

The municipality may fix and prescribe penalties for the violation of such an ordinance or of any section thereof in a sum not exceeding twenty-five dollars or imprisonment for a period not exceeding ten days in the municipal lock-up or county jail as may be designated by the governing body. The words "beauty culture," in connection with these parlors, are defined in the Act.82 81

XVII. CHATTEL MORTGAGES Section 50. Security. 

51. Application of Act. 

52. Affidavit of Consideration. 

53. Recordation. 

§ 50. Security. The essence of a chattel mortgage is the security it gives for the payment of a debt.83 A chattel mortgage securing several notes secures them all equally. 85 

§ 51. Application of Act. The object of the Chattel 

Mortgage Act has been said to be to publicize all liens on personalty the possession of which is retained by the mortgagor. The Act, the court has held, applies to all chattel mortgages, there being no special types recognized by, or exempt from, its provisions.85 See section 

53 on this point. § 52. Affidavit of Consideration. A chattel mortgage not being accompanied by immediate delivery of the mortgaged chattels, the affidavit attached to the mortgage need state only the consideration in order to protect subsequent purchasers and mortgagees in good faith.86 

However, a substantial deviation from the truth in the affidavit, however honestly made, renders the mortgage void as against creditors.87 "The consideration must not

It is to be noted that, when a chattel mortgage pertains to the goods, merchandise, or other chattels acquired or held by a mortgagor engaged in the business of manufacturing, processing, storing, shipping, or selling of such goods, merchandise, or other chattels, the mortgage need not be recorded, providing that, prior to the execution of any such mortgage or series of mortgages, the mortgagor and mortgagee execute under seal and acknowledge a statement, setting forth the location of the chief place of business of each of the parties, the fact that the mortgagor may execute and the mortgagee may accept a series of chattel-mortgage transactions resulting from the business of the mortgagor, and a general description of the type of chattels to be made the subject of the mortgages.99 This statement is to be recorded and indexed among the chattel-mortgage records in the manner in which chattel mortgages are required to be recorded and indexed.89 No affidavit or affirmation need be annexed to such statement.89

XVIII. COAL AND OTHER FUELS Section 54. Transportation of Coal. 

55. Solid Fuels. 

56. Liquid Fuels. 

§ 54. Transportation of Coal. A violation of the An thracite Coal Act of 1937, as amended in 1938, is visited with a penalty of not less than one hundred dollars and not more than five hundred dollars.⁹0 

The procedure for the recovery of a penalty incurred under the statute, which concerns the transportation of anthracite coal into this State by motor vehicles is the same as the procedure set forth in section 51:1-105 et seq. of the Revised Statutes.91 The action is instituted in the name of the  by any weights-andmeasures officer, who possesses the same powers in connection with enforcing this Act as those vested in him under the said sections."1 Jurisdiction over such cases is vested in justices of the peace, judges of the city criminal courts, police judges, and recorders located in the county wherein the violation has been committed.º¹ 

Should the defendant not be able to pay the fine imposed, he is to be committed to the county jail for a period of not more than ninety days.⁹ 90 

§ 55. Solid Fuels. In 1937 and 1939 extensive provi sions were enacted with respect to the licensing of dealers, weighing, and so forth of solid fuels.* Jurisdiction over the offenses thereunder has been conferred on justices of the peace, judges of the city criminal courts, re-

XIX. COMMERCE AND NAVIGATION Section 57. Power Vessels. 

§ 57. Power Vessels. When a complaint is made in writing, duly verified, that a person has violated any of the provisions of the 1938 statute governing the operating, docking, mooring, and anchoring of power vessels within the confines of this State's inland waterways, a magistrate of the county or a recorder or police magistrate of the municipality in which the offense has been committed may, within thirty days after the commission of the offense, issue either a summons or a warrant directed to any constable, police officer, or harbor master appointed under the Act for the appearance or arrest of the defendant.97 This complaint must state the provision or section of the Act alleged to have been violated as well as the time, place, and nature of the violation." 

These proceedings may be instituted on any day of the week; 98 process served on Sunday is as valid as that served on any other day.98

The complaint in the prosecution may be in this form: Before Recorder. 

Henry Sands, Prosecutor,

Edward Runner, Defendant.

The summons or warrant issued by the magistrate is valid throughout the entire State.99 An officer who possesses the power to serve the summons or to serve the warrant and make the arrest thereunder in the county wherein the process is issued may exercise such powers in any of the several counties of the State.99 In the case of a corporate defendant, a summons is issued requiring the corporation to be and appear before the judicial officer on the date therein named to answer the complaint.¹ 

Such summons is to be served on the president, vicepresident, secretary, superintendent, or manager of the corporation or on the captain or acting captain of any boat, dredge, scow, or other craft by which the offense has been committed, at least five days prior to the time and appearance named therein.¹

On the return of the summons or warrant, the magistrate or recorder proceeds, with or without a jury, to hear the case and determine the defendant's guilt or in- 

99. Same, p. 173, R.S. 12:7–42. 

1. Same, p. 172, R.S. 12:7-38.

COMMERCE AND NAVIGATION 

93. nocence. However, the defendant may, on the return day of the process, demand and have a trial by a jury of twelve men, the defendant, at the time of the demand, paying the costs of all the proceedings of the jury trial.* 

Should the defendant make a request therefor, the hearing is to be adjourned for a period not exceeding thirty days from the return date named in the summons or from the date of the arrest without warrant as the case may be. Under such circumstances, it is the duty of the magistrate or recorder to detain the defendant in safe custody, unless he makes a cash deposit or enters into a bond with the State with at least one sufficient surety to all and in an amount not exceeding five hundred dollars, conditioned for his appearance on the day to which the hearing is adjourned and thence from day to day until the disposition of the cause. Instead of giving the cash deposit or bond, the defendant may leave with the judicial officer the vessel owned or operated by such person or corporation at the time of the commission of the offense. If forfeited, the cash deposit is to be paid to the Board of Commerce and Navigation by the magistrate or recorder; a suit on the said bond may be prosecuted by the Board or by the Attorney-General of the 

State in any court of competent jurisdiction.³

The penalty for a violation of the Act is a fine not exceeding one hundred dollars. Should this fine not be paid, a sentence of imprisonment in the county jail for a period not exceeding ten days is to be imposed.* A second conviction of the same offense results in a fine in double the amount prescribed for the first offense or, in default of the payment thereof, imprisonment for a period not exceeding twenty days.*

An appeal may be made under this Act to the court of

XX. CONSTABLES Section 58. Number and Qualifications. 

59. Term. 

60. Fee Paid by Constable. 

§ 58. Number and Qualifications. The number of con stables to be elected by a municipality depends on the class of the particular municipality: in first-class cities, the governing body may annually, by resolution, appoint not more than one hundred and fifty constables, each of whom must have been a resident and a registered voter of the city for at least three years prior to the appointment; in all cities other than first- or third-class cities, there may be one constable in each ward except as otherwise provided in the city charters; in third-class cities and in towns, there may be as many constables in each ward as the number of justices of the peace to which the ward is entitled; in townships, there are to be as many constables as the number of justices of the peace to which the township is entitled, and there are to be two constables in boroughs.* 

§ 59. Term. The constable's term of office is three years, with the exception that in cities of the first class he holds office for one year." 

§ 60. Fee Paid by Constable. The governing body of a first-class city may, by resolution, charge each constable an annual fee of not more than fifty dollars. Such body is to supply him with a suitably inscribed badge, and the badge is to be returned to the city at the expiration of his term of office.

7. Same, R.S. 40:41-36.

XXI. CONTEMPT Section 61. Power of Court. 

62. Civil and Criminal Contempt. 

63. Acts Constituting Contempt. 

64. Proof. 

65. Inability to Comply with Court Order. 

§ 61. Power of Court. The court's power to punish contempt is an inherent one and is essential to the execution of its powers and the maintenance of its dignity and authority. This power should be exercised, whenever necessary, in the interest of justice.⁹ 

The court's power in this matter extends to all who injuriously interfere with the proper exercise of judicial functions, whether the persons guilty be officers of the court, parties, or strangers.10 The punishment for contempt lies within the court's discretion and may consist of a fine, imprisonment, or both and may amount to a virtual confiscation of the guilty party's estate.10 

§ 62. Civil and Criminal Contempt. A civil contempt consists in the failure to do an act ordered by the court for the benefit of the opposing party in a civil proceeding, while criminal contempt may be said to be conduct directed against the dignity and authority of the court and may occur in a civil or criminal action or in a special proceeding."1 

§ 63. Acts Constituting Contempt. Any unlawful in terference with the subject-matter of litigation consti-

97 tutes contempt of court.12 Unwarranted public criticism of the decision of a court, instead of the pursuit of the statutory method for the correction of the alleged errors in the decision, has been held to constitute contempt in parties and counsel.10 Contemptuous actions, though committed without the courtroom, if admitted in open court, constitute contempt in facie curiæ.10 

The contemptuous actions of a layman deserve greater punishment when committed by an attorney.10 An attorney who advises or participates in such an action is guilty of contempt.' 10 14 § 64. Proof. It has been said that criminal contempt must be proved by clear and convincing testimony¹³ beyond a reasonable doubt. However, it is sufficient if the acts tend to obstruct the administration of justice and unlawfully interfere with the subject-matter of litigation.18 

§ 65. Inability to Comply with Court Order. Inability to obey an order of the court is a good defense to a charge of contempt.15

§ 66. Delivery. A deed may not be delivered in escrow to the grantee or obligee named therein.16 

A deed is presumed to be delivered on the day of the date of the acknowledgement thereof.¹ 

§ 67. Capacity of Parties. The test for determining the mental capacity to execute and deliver a deed lies in the question whether the grantor has possessed sufficient mind to understand in a reasonable manner the nature and effect of his act." 18 § 68. Fraud. That the consideration for a deed is expressed to be one dollar is not sufficient to establish fraud.19 § 69. Rights under a Conveyance. XXIII. COSTS Section 71. Right to Recover Costs. 

72. Enforcing Payment. 

24 

§ 71. Right to Recover Costs. The right to costs is statutory.23 However, in the absence of statutory enactment, the matter lies within the discretion of the court. 

The allowance of costs has been said to be governed by the law in effect at the time of the termination of the action.25

No costs are allowed when both parties are partly successful on the defendant's appeal.26

An action or proceeding of any nature being commenced in any court against an officer or enlisted man, either for an act done by the officer in his official capacity in the discharge of his duties or for an alleged omission to do an act which it is his duty to perform, or against an officer or enlisted man acting under proper authority for an act done in pursuance thereof or by a warrant issued pursuant to law, the defendant may require the person instituting or prosecuting the action to file security for the payment of costs in such amount and in such form as the judge of the court in which the proceeding has been instituted may determine.26a Should the plaintiff be nonsuited or have a verdict or judgment rendered against him, the defendant then recovers treble costs.26a

§ 72. Enforcing Payment. When costs are an inci dent to a common-law judgment, the payment thereof can be enforced only by the ordinary process of execution:" the costs are considered to be a part of the judgment."7 

There being no statutory authority on the matter, an interlocutory order for the payment of costs is enforceable, not by execution, but by common-law attachment for contempt."

XXIV. CRIMES Section 73. Crimes of Infants. 

74. Treason. 

75. Kidnaping. 

76. Bigamy. 

77. Death Caused by Driving. 

78. Motor Vehicles. 

79. False Swearing. 

80. Obtaining Bills and Notes. 

§ 73. Crimes of Infants. The rule in this State today is that a person under the age of sixteen years is deemed incapable of committing a crime either under the common law or the statutes of this State.28 § 74. Treason. Anyone owing allegiance to this State who levies war against it or adheres to its enemies or to the enemies of the United States, by giving them or any of them any aid or comfort whatsoever within this State or elsewhere and who is convicted thereof on the testimony of two witnesses to the same overt act whereof he stands indicted or on confession in open court is guilty of treason and is to suffer death.29 § 75. Kidnaping. Kidnaping constitutes a high misdemeanor in this State, punishable by imprisonment for life or for a period of not less than thirty years.30 To demand ransom in connection with the kidnaping renders the crime punishable by death, unless the jury, by its verdict and as a part thereof and on and after the consideration of all the evidence, recommends imprisonment at hard labor for life, in which case such punishment and no greater punishment is to be imposed.80 

28. 1 Rev. 1937, R.S. 2:103-3.1. 

29. Same, R.S. 2:173-1. 

30. Same, R.S. 2:143-1. 102

CRIMES 

103 Threatening or attempting to kidnap is also a high misdemeanor, the punishment for which is imprisonment at hard labor for a period not exceeding thirty years or a fine of not more than five thousand dollars or both.31 § 76. Bigamy. A person who, having a husband or wife living, marries another is guilty of bigamy and is to be punished by a fine not exceeding one thousand dollars or imprisonment at hard labor for a period not exceeding ten years or both.32 This statute does not extend to any person whose spouse continually remains without the United States for the period of five years together; any person whose spouse absents himself or herself, the one from the other, for the period of five years together, the party remarrying being ignorant of the fact that the other has been living within that time; any person who is, at the time of such marriage, divorced by the judgment or decree of any authority or court having cognizance thereof, or any person whose former marriage has been, by the judgment or decree of any such authority or court, declared to be void and of no effect.32 

Any person who marries another's spouse, knowing that the circumstances of the marriage are such that the said spouse by reason of such marriage is guilty of bigamy is guilty of a misdemeanor.33 

§ 77. Death Caused by Driving. To cause the death of another by driving a vehicle carelessly and heedlessly in willful or wanton disregard of the rights or safety of others is to commit a misdemeanor.84 

§ 78. Motor Vehicles. To steal any motor vehicle or to take it unlawfully or to be an accessory to such actions is a high misdemeanor, punishable by a fine of not more

He who purchases or receives a motor vehicle, knowing it to be a stolen one, is guilty of a high misdemeanor, punishable in the same manner.36 

A high misdemeanor is committed by one who maliciously burns a motor vehicle, and the punishment therefor is a fine not exceeding one thousand dollars or imprisonment for a period not exceeding five years or both." § 79. False Swearing. A person, his procurers, aiders, and abettors who willfully swear falsely in a judicial proceeding or willfully swear falsely before anyone authorized by the laws of this State to administer oaths and acting within his authority are guilty of false swearing.38 

False swearing is a misdemeanor.39

Within the scope of these provisions, an affirmation has the same effect as an oath, and the word "willful" means intentional and knowing the same to be false.40 

§ 80. Obtaining Bills and Notes. The stealing, taking by robbery, or obtaining possession by false pretenses, representations, or promises, of any bank bill or note, bill of exchange, order, warrant, draft, check, bond, bill, or promissory note for the payment of money, or any certificate or other public security of the United States or of this State or of any other state of the Union for the payment of money, or acknowledging the receipt of money or goods, being the property of any other person, notwithstanding that the said particulars or any of them is or may be termed in law a chose in action, is a high misdemeanor.41

XXV. CRIMINAL PROCEDURE Section 81. Courts in First-Class Cities. 

82. Alibis. 

83. Compromises. 

84. Extradition.

85a. Oaths and Affirmations.

When a person § 81. Courts in First-Class Cities. 

charged with any crime or offense enumerated in this section waives, in writing, indictment and trial by jury, the criminal court in a first-class city, in addition to its other powers, possesses the jurisdiction to try and determine, whenever any of the following crimes or offenses has been committed within the corporate limits of the city:42 a. All cases of assault, assault and battery, desertion and nonsupport, malicious mischief, larceny from the person, larceny and embezzlement where the amount alleged to have been taken is not more than two hundred dollars; b. Overdrawing bank account and obtaining money or property under false pretenses, where the amount alleged to have been taken is not more than two hundred dollars; c. Receiving stolen property, where the property alleged to have been received is not more than two hundred dollars in value; d. Taking and driving an automobile without the consent of the owner, and e. Other criminal offenses, where the penalty that may be imposed does not exceed a fine of five hundred dollars or imprisonment for a term not exceeding one year or both.42 

42. Same, R.S. 2:219-33. 105

§ 82. Alibis. In an instance where the defendant in a criminal case intends to rely in any way on an alibi, he must, on the prosecuting officer's written demand, furnish a written bill of particulars, signed by him, with respect to the specific place or places where the defendant claims to have been at the time of the commission of the alleged offense and the names and addresses of the witnesses on whom he intends to rely in establishing the alibi.43 Such particulars are to be given to the prosecuting officer within five days from the service of the demand.43 

Within two days after he has received the aforesaid bill of particulars, the said officer must, on written demand therefor, furnish the defendant or his attorney a written bill of particulars as to the names and addresses of the witnesses on whom the State intends to rely in establishing the defendant's presence at the scene of the allegd offense.43 44 The trial court possesses the power to order the amendment, amplification, or reduction of these particulars or of the time of their service as the ends of justice may require. The party neglecting to furnish the bills as required may be denied the right to present any evidence at the trial as to the defendant's absence from, or presence at, the scene of the alleged offense, or the court may impose such conditions and grant such adjournments as the ends of justice may require.44 § 83. Compromises. If a judgment has been entered on a forfeited recognizance or bail bond in a criminal case in any court and if it appears to the court that the person accused or indicted has surrendered or has been returned to the custody of the court in which he has been accused or indicted or to the proper officers thereof, the court may, in its discretion, approve a compromise and 

43. 1 Rev. 1937, R.S. 2:190-7. 

44. Same, R.S. 2:190-8.

CRIMINAL PROCEDURE 

107 settlement of the judgment and order the judgment to be cancelled of record.45 § 84. Extradition. When anyone in this State. is charged on the oath of a credible person before a judge or magistrate with the commission of a crime in another state and with having fled from justice, except in the case of the extradition of a person not present in the demanding state at the time of the commission of the crime, or with being convicted of crime in that state and escaping from confinement or with breaking the terms of his bail, probation, or parole or when a complaint has been made before any judge or magistrate in this State setting forth on the affidavit of any credible person in another state that a crime has been committed in such other state and that the accused has been charged therewith and, excepting in the particular case above mentioned, has fled from justice or with being convicted of a crime in that state and escaping from confinement or with breaking the terms of his bail, probation, or parole and is believed to be in this State, the judge or magistrate is to issue a warrant directed to any police officer commanding him to apprehend the person named therein, wherever he may be found in this State, and to bring such person before him or any other judge or magistrate available in, or convenient of access to, the place where the arrest may be made, to answer the charge or the complaint or affidavit.46

A certified copy of the sworn charge or complaint and affidavit on which the warrant is issued is to be attached to the warrant.46

If, on the examination before the judge or magistrate, it appears that the person held is the one charged and, except in the case above noted, that he has fled from justice, the judge or magistrate must, by a warrant reciting

CRIMINAL PROCEDURE 

109 When the accused has not been arrested on the Governor's warrant by the expiration of the time fixed in the warrant, bond, or undertaking, a judge or magistrate may either discharge him or recommit him for a further period of sixty days, or a Supreme Court Justice or a county judge may again take bail for his appearance and surrender as above provided but within a period not to exceed sixty days after the date of such new bond or undertaking.49

On the accused's failure to appear and surrender himself in accord with the conditions of the bond, the judge or magistrate, by a proper order, is to declare the bond forfeited and to order the immediate arrest of the accused if he be within this State.50 There may then be a recovery on the bond in the name of the State as in the case of other bonds or undertakings given by the accused in criminal proceedings in this State." 50 The guilt or innocence of the accused with regard to the crime with which he stands charged is not a proper matter of inquiry in these proceedings, except insofar as such question may be involved in identifying the person held as the individual charged with the crime.51

XXVI. DISORDERLY PERSONS Section 86. Jurisdiction. 

87. Soliciting Funds. 

88. Convict Goods. 

88a. Simulation of Court Process. 

89. Alcoholic Beverages. 

90. Inability to Account for Oneself and Other Disorderly Acts. 

91. Motor-Vehicle Insurance. 

92. Barbers. 

93. Sale of Funeral Articles. 

94. Throwing Articles at Vehicles. 

95. Poppies and Forget-Me-Nots. 

96. Decisions. 

§ 86. Jurisdiction. To warrant the conviction of a person as a disorderly person, he must have committed an offense against the Disorderly Persons Act.53

When a violation of such Act occurs on any highway or at any place in a county wherein there has been established a county traffic court, such court possesses jurisdiction to try and determine the case. In an instance where a municipality possesses a police court, criminal court, or recorder's court, persons arrested for a violation of the Act concerning disorderly persons are to be taken before such court for a hearing.54 No justice of the peace is vested with the power to hear, try, or determine these cases in such a municipality, any law, custom, or usage to the contrary notwithstanding.54a

It has recently been held that a borough recorder has the power to impose a sentence of six months for a violation of the Disorderly Persons Act.55 

53. Breisia v. Court of Common Pleas, 11 N. J. Misc. 937, 169 Atl. Rep. 835. 

54. 1 Rev. 1937, R.S. 2:229-3. 

54a. P. L. 1939, ch. 250. 

55. State v. O'Donnell, 120 N. J. L. 394, 200 Atl. Rep. 739. 112

DISORDERLY PERSONS 

113 § 87. Soliciting Funds. To solicit funds or contributions for any organization of law-enforcement officers is unlawful, and the person guilty of such an act is deemed to be a disorderly person.56 However, the members of such an organization may personally solicit funds or contributions in the municipality in which they are employed or, in the case of a county or state organization, throughout the county or State in which they are employed.56 

The offense is said to be committed in the county in which the person solicited has been at the time of the solicitation, though such solicitation may have been made by telephone and may have originated outside the county or may have been made by mail deposited in a postoffice outside the county.56 

§ 88. Convict Goods. Goods, wares and merchandise, manufactured or produced by convicts or prisoners in another state are, on arrival or delivery in this State for use, consumption, sale, or storage, to be subject to the laws of this State with respect to goods made in penal institutions;57 no exemption from such effect may be based on the facts of the goods' being brought into the 

State in the original package.57

A person, firm, or corporation violating this statute is a disorderly person and, on conviction, is to be punished by a fine of not less than fifty dollars and not more than five hundred dollars or imprisonment for not less than thirty days and not more than ninety days or both.57 The procedure to be followed under this Act is that outlined on page 194 of the original text of this book.57 

§ 88a. Simulation of Court Process. A person, firm, or corporation or any officer, agent, or employee thereof, who prints for sale or distribution or who circulates, distributes, publishes, or offers for sale any paper or docu- 

56. 1938 Stat. Serv., p. 107, R.S. 2:202-16.2. 

57. 2 Rev. 1937, R.S. 46:31-6. 

[Honeyman's Justice Supp.]-8

ment or any blank form of paper or document which, when filled out, simulates or is intended to simulate any summons, complaint, writ, or court process of any kind is to be adjudged a disorderly person and is to receive the punishment provided by section 2:205-1 of the Re vised Statutes.57a 

§ 89. Alcoholic Beverages. One who misrepresents or misstates his age or the age of another for the purpose of inducing a licensee or the employee of a licensee to sell, serve, or deliver an alcoholic beverage to a person under the age of twenty-one years is a disorderly person and, on conviction, is to be punished by a fine of not more than two hundred dollars.58 The procedure to be followed is that set forth on page 194 of the original text of this work.58 

§ 90. Inability to Account for Oneself and Other Dis orderly Acts. A person apprehended either on foot or in an automobile, motor vehicle, or public conveyance, who cannot give a good account of himself or who is engaged in an illegal occupation and who is in this State for an unlawful purpose is to be adjudged a disorderly person.59 

That such individual cannot give a good account of himself or that he is engaged in an illegal occupation is prima-facie evidence in the prosecution that he is in the 

State for an unlawful purpose. 59 When one is apprehended and is proved, to the satisfaction of the magistrate, recorder, or judge before whom he is brought, to be a person who is engaged in an illegal occuption or who bears a bad reputation and consorts for an unlawful purpose with thieves, burglars, pickpockets, swindlers, confidence men, or other criminals or persons who bear a bad reputation, the person appre- 

57a. P. L. 1939, ch. 275. 

58. Same, R.S. 33:1-81. 

59. 1 Rev. 1937, R.S. 2:202-16. 

[Honeyman's Justice Supp.]

DISORDERLY PERSONS 

115 hended is to be adjudged a disorderly person.59 The fact that such person is engaged in an illegal occupation or bears a bad reputation and is found consorting with such characters is prima-facie evidence in the prosecution that such consorting has been for an unlawful purpose.59 

However, an isolated meeting in a parked automobile for thirty minutes on a certain morning has been held not to constitute "consorting."

He who has in his possession or custody any lotteryslips, books, or records pertaining to a lottery or he who has in his posssession or has in an automobile in his custody any ticket or tickets, slip or slips, paper, document, or memoranda in any way pertaining to the business of a number game is to be adjudged a disorderly person.59 

The words "number game" may be said to pertain to any betting on any number or numbers or sets or arrangements of numbers on or according to any plan or method whatsoever.5 59 

§ 91. Motor-Vehicle Insurance. The driver of a motor vehicle who fails or refuses to exhibit, on the request of a proper person, the evidence of insurance or of other financial coverage provided for by statute is a disorderly person. He is liable, on conviction, to a penalty of not more than one hundred dollars or imprisonment for a period of not more than ninety days or both in the magistrate's discretion."1 § 92. Barbers. Anyone violating any of the provisions of the Act with respect to the regulation and registration of barbers is to be adjudged a disorderly person and, on conviction before a police justice or recorder or a court of competent jurisdiction of this State, is to be fined not more than one hundred dollars.62 

59. 1 Rev. 1937, R.S. 2:202-16.

§ 93. Sale of Funeral Articles. A person who, for pe cuniary gain, solicits any person in the house of a family in which a death has recently occurred in order to sell or to obtain orders for funeral wreaths, flowers, or like merchandise is a disorderly person. The punishment is the same as that set forth on page 195 of the original text of this work." 63 63 An appeal from a conviction for violating this section does not act as a stay of the execution of the judgment or sentence, unless the magistrate or a Supreme Court Justice certifies that there is reasonable ground to believe that the conviction is erroneous, in which case alone does the appeal operate as a stay.64 

§ 94. Throwing Articles at Vehicles. He who casts or throws any stick, stone, pebble, or other substance or who shoots from an air gun, machine gun, shot gun, pistol, or rifle any shot or ball at, against, or into a trolley car, street-railway car, steam-railroad car, electricrailroad car, railroad locomotive, motor omnibus, or motor vehicle or he who places any stick, stone, or other substance on a street-railway track, trolley track, or railroad track, with the intent to injure any car passing thereon or the passengers therein or he who unlawfully climbs into or on any coal car, either in motion or standing at any of the depots or stations of any railroad company in this State, and throws any coal therefrom or takes from the track or alongside the tracks any coal so thrown from the cars is to be adjudged a disorderly person.65 

§ 95. Poppies and Forget-Me-Nots. The selling or offering for sale of any poppy or poppies, forget-me-not or forget-me-nots, except as provided in the Acts with respect to such flowers, is to be construed as presump- 63. 1 Rev. 1937, R.S. 2:202-16.1.

A defendant riding in a taxicab and having no fixed dwelling place and travelling around without visible means of support is a "vagabond" under the Disorderly Persons Act. 67 

A person who has been previously convicted of two assaults and a violation of the White Slave Act and who is seeking dope at the time of his arrest is not a "common thief, burglar or pickpocket" within the scope of the Act.68 

To call a person "rat" and "scum of the earth" violates the Act. Interference with a contractor and a railroad company engaged in eliminating a grade crossing constitutes disorderly conduct.70

When one is charged with being a disorderly person within the meaning of the statute with regard thereto, he is entitled to be confronted with proofs of the matters charged."¹

110a. Use of Memoranda. § 97. General Principles. The general rule is that evidence is admissible if it is relevant, material, and competent.72 Testimony may be said to be relevant, the court has said in a recent case, when the fact to which it is applied is so related to a certain material fact that, according to the common course of events, the existence of the first fact proves or renders probable the existence of the second.73

The best evidence obtainable must be given.74 Should it appear from the nature of the case that positive proof may be obtained, such proof is required.75

In order that testimony may be worthy of belief, it must be offered by a credible witness and must be such as the common experience and observation of reasonable 

72. Hampton v. Pennsylvania Rr. Co., 115 N. J. L. 168, 179 Atl. Rep. 101. 

73. Fisher v. Nutley, 120 N. J. L. 290, 199 Atl. Rep. 40. 

74. Pluchino v. Piccolo, 114 N. J. L. 82, 175 Atl. Rep. 812. 

75. Belyus v. Wilkinson, Gaddis & Co., 115 N. J. L. 43, 178 Atl. Rep. 

181; aff. 116 N. J. L. 92, 182 Atl. Rep. 873. 118

EVIDENCE 

119 men can approve as probable under the circumstances.76 

Improbable testimony may be said to be such as imputes conduct inconsistent with the principles by which persons similarly situated are governed.76 When a witness' testimony is inconsistent with the common principles by which the conduct of mankind is naturally governed, he is not entitled to credit, and the court may disbelieve him whenever there is reason therefor."7

A conviction of an individual as a disorderly person is not admissible to impeach his credibility as a witness.78 

That one has been committed to the state hospital for the insane does not necessarily disqualify him from acting as a witness, especially in an instance where he has not been adjudged insane by a lunacy commission." 79 

§ 98. Burden of Proof. The burden of proof rests on the plaintiff and never shifts to the defendant.80 However, the defendant must either submit evidence to offset the plaintiff's prima-facie case or suffer the rendering of a judgment against him.80 The duty of going forward with the proof of new matters rests on the defendant.80 

Where the evidence adduced by both sides is equally persuasive, the jury must render its verdict for the defendant.80

A verdict may be founded on circumstances alone.81 

Circumstantial or presumptive evidence, in the determination of civil causes, may be said to be a mere preponderance of probabilities and is, therefore, a sufficient basis of decision.81
