LIX. SET-OFFS AND COUNTERCLAIMS Section 217. Construction of Statute. 

94 

218. Effect. 

§ 217. Construction of Statute. Statutes with respect to set-offs and counterclaims are to be construed liberally. In doing so, the courts generally depart from the rigorous language of the statute and freely apply equitable principles.95 § 218. Effect. As a general rule, the effect of a set-off is to admit the plaintiff's cause of action and to urge a counter-demand having an origin independent of the cause on which the suit is based.96 "It is really a counter or cross action and does not defend against the merits of the claim which the plaintiff makes." 

94. McCurnin v. Realty Co., 11 N. J. Misc. 673, 168 Atl. Rep. 161. 

95. Newman v. Hatfield Wire & Cable Co., 113 N. J. L. 484, 174 Atl. 

Rep. 491. 

96. Cook v. Soden, 12 N. J. Misc. 337, 171 Atl. Rep. 558. 228

LX. STATE PROPERTY Section 219. Wrongful Acts Committed on State Property. 

§ 219. Wrongful Acts Committed on State Property. 

The following acts committed on any of the state properties under the jurisdiction of the Board of Conservation and Development entail a penalty of not less than twentyfive dollars and not more than one hundred fifty dollars for each offense:97 the abuse, mutilation, injury, removal, or destruction of any living plant or animal, or any structure or other physical features or properties on such lands; the indulging in conduct or language which is indecent or unduly annoying to other visitors; the using of the public conveniences or facilities on any such lands contrary to the instructions or directions of the Board's representatives in charge of such lands; the starting of a fire on such lands, or the disrobing for bathing excepting under such conditions as are authorized by the Board's representatives in charge of such lands or in bath houses provided for that purpose. Written permission of the Board or its representative to commit an act mentioned in the first, fourth, or fifth group is sufficient to preclude the imposition of the penalty." 97 The procedure in such cases is the same as that used in prosecutions based on forest fires." See page 276 of the original text of this work.98 97. 1 Rev. 1937, R.S. 13:1-26. 

98. See also 1 Rev. 1937, R.S. 13:9-1, et seq., as to the procedure. 229

LXI. TRIAL Section 220. Court's Power over Trial. 

221. Court's Discretion. 

222. Judge Sitting without Jury. 

223. Evidence. 

224. Cross-Examination. 

§ 220. Court's Power over Trial. The conducting of a trial is a matter which rests in the sound discretion of the trial judge;99 thus, a motion for a continuance is addressed to the discretion of the court.¹

The control of the direct and cross-examination is within the province of the trial judge, in order that the proofs may be kept within reasonable bounds.* 

A court clerk or deputy clerk possesses no power to poll the jury in a civil suit.2a § 221. Court's Discretion. The lower court's exercise of discretion will not be reviewed by the appellate court, unless the discretion has been clearly abused. An abuse of discretion is not shown in an instance where the action taken by the lower court is in furtherance of justice and does not violate accepted principles.3 "The fundamental rule is that in granting or refusing a new trial the trial court exercises a discretionary power and that its discretion is not ordinarily reviewable on appeal." LXII. VICE AND IMMORALITY Section 225. Giving Bond on Sunday. 

§ 225. Giving Bond on Sunday. The giving of a bond on a Sunday, to obtain the release of one imprisoned in a civil proceeding, has been held to be a work of charity within the purview of the Sunday laws, and the bond is valid.10 

10. Ballentine v. Ballentine, 123 N. J. E. 577, 199 Atl. Rep. 423. 232

LXIII. WEIGHTS AND MEASURES Section 226. Measuring Devices. 

227. Grain, Coal, Etc. 

228. Lumber. 

229. Actions. 

§ 226. Measuring Devices. Anyone violating any of the provisions of the statute with respect to condemned, rebuilt, and used weighing or measuring devices is to be fined not less than twenty-five dollars and not more than fifty dollars for the first offense and not less than fifty dollars and not more than one hundred dollars for the second offense and not less than one hundred dollars and not more than two hundred dollars for any subsequent offense.¹¹ A person who is unable to pay the fine is to be committed to jail for a period of not less than ten days and not more than ninety days.¹ 11 The action to recover the penalty is to be brought in the name of the  by any duly appointed weights-and-measures officer on a written complaint, duly verified by such officer and the verification may be made on information and belief or verified by the complaining witness, to be filed with the magistrate of any municipality, alleging the violation in the municipality of any provision of the Act.12 

11. 1938 Stat. Serv., p. 555, R.S. 51:1-131. 

12. Same, p. 555, R.S. 51:1-132. 233

The complant may take the following form: 

Before 

, Robert Lark, Prosecutor,

V. 

COMPLAINT. 

Lemuel Samuels, Defendant. 

State of New Jersey 

County of Camden SS. 

Robert Lark, being duly sworn according to law, on his oath complains in the name of the State of New Jersey that he is a superintendent of weights and measures for the State of New Jersey; that the defendant, Lemuel Samuels, is engaged in the Borough of Audubon, N. J., in the selling of second-hand weighing devices, and that the said defendant has never obtained a license so to do, contrary to the provisions of section two of an Act entitled "An Act to license and regulate persons engaging in the business of selling, trading-in, receiving, installing, or repairing condemned, rebuilt or used weighing or measuring devices, and providing penalties for the violation thereof," approved May 14th, 1938. Wherefore, the complainant asks that the said Lemuel Samuels be summoned or apprehended and dealt with in accord with the Act. Sworn and subscribed at 

Audubon, N. J., on the 5th day of July, 1939, before me,

ROBERT LARK. 

The magistrate may issue a summons returnable in one to ten days from the date thereof,-which is to be served not less than two days from the return thereof, or a warrant directed to any weights-and-measures officer or to any constable or police officer commanding him to cause the accused to be summoned or arrested and brought before the magistrate, who, on the return of the process, forthwith summarily hears the case and determines the

WEIGHTS AND MEASURES 

235 guilt or innocence of the accused and, on a conviction, imposes the penalty prescribed together with the costs.12 

Should the defendant fail to pay the penalty and costs, execution is to issue against his goods and chattels or against his body without first obtaining an order from the court for that purpose.12 When the officer executing the writ is unable to find enough of the defendant's goods and chattels in his bailiwick to satisfy the sum due, the officer is to take the defendant's body and to deliver him to the keeper of the county jail for a period not to exceed ten days, excepting as otherwise provided in this Act, or until the sum due has been sooner paid.12

The defendant may be released on the magistrate's order before the expiration of any imprisonment prescribed in default of the payment of the judgment, on the written order of the committing magistrate or of a Supreme Court Justice, in the discretion of the said judicial officer, after the giving of one day's notice of the time and place of the application to the State Superin tendent of Weights and Measures.12 

The city attorney of any municipality wherein the violation takes place must assist in the prosecution of the action, unless the municipality has no such municipal superintendent of weights and measures in which case the county prosecutor assists in the prosecution.12

The fines and penalties recovered are to be paid by the magistrate, when recovered by a state weights-andmeasures officer, to the State Treasurer; when recovered by a county weights-and-measures officer, to the county collector, and, when recovered by a municipal weightsand-measures officer, to the municipality represented by such officer.12

Any weights-and-measures officer within whose view a violation of this Act is committed may, without a war-

rant, arrest the offender and take him before any magistrate of the county.12 The magistrate must, on the filing of a written and verified complaint setting forth the nature of the offense, to be filed then and there with the magistrate before the commencement of the hearing, summarily hear the case and determine the guilt or innocence of the accused and then, on a conviction, inflict the prescribed penalties.12

The Act makes a number of provisions with respect to the appeal in these cases.12 

§ 227. Grain, Coal, Etc. Anyone violating the statute with regard to the weighing of grain, coal, charcoal, and so forth is liable to a penalty of not less than twenty-five dollars and not more than fifty dollars for the first offense and not less than fifty dollars and not more than one hundred dollars for the second offense and not less than one hundred dollars and not more than two hundred dollars for each subsequent offense.13 

§ 228. Lumber. Any deviation from the authorized standards of grade, measure, and trade-name of lumber and lumber products, when such deviation indicates an intention to defraud, is visited with a penalty of not less than twenty-five dollars and not more than fifty dollars for the first offense and not less than fifty dollars and not more than one hundred dollars for the second offense and not less than one hundred dollars and not more than two hundred dollars for each subsequent offense or imprisonment for not less than thirty days and not more than ninety days or both, the amount of the said penalty being determined in the discretion of the district court or police magistrate possessing jurisdiction over the matter.14 

13. 2 Rev. 1937, R.S. 51:1-38. 

14. Same, R.S. 51:4-22.

WEIGHTS AND MEASURES 

237 Should the defendant fail to pay the penalty imposed and the costs of the prosecution, the judicial officer is to issue execution against the goods and chattels and against the body of the defendant as provided in the Act.¹ 

14 However, the period of detention of ten days provided for in the Act is in this case to be extended to a period of not more than ninety days.14

The action to recover this penalty may be brought in the name of the  by a lumber inspector, the procedure being the same as though the action were brought by a superintendent.¹4 § 229. Actions. The superintendents and assistant superintendents of weights and measures possess the authority to bring actions or proceedings in their official capacities within their respective jurisdictions in order to enforce penalties.15

The action for the recovery of a penalty for the violation of the Act anent weights, measures, and containers is in the nature of an action at law and may be brought in the district court or in the small cause court or in the court of a police magistrate or recorder.16 Excepting as otherwise provided in the Act, the practice is the same as that in other actions in the same court.16 

The penalties recovered are paid to the State Treasurer, when the action is brought by the state superintendent or any of his assistant superintendents; to the county treasurer, when the action is brought by the county superintendent or an assistant county superintendent, and to the treasurer of the municipality, when the action is brought by a municipal superintendent or an assistant municipal superintendent."
